**★ ② HBM4 COST RE-MARKED AND — the part that matters for competitive positioning — IT IS TIERED (Jefferies/Fubon group call · Sherman Shang, 2026-08-03).** **"NVDA's HBM4 cost from $17-18/GB [HBM3e] to $30-32/GB in 2026… For other GPU and CUSTOM-ASIC projects, we believe their HBM cost will be HIGHER AT US$35-36 PER GB, meaning THE HBM COST WILL DOUBLE IN 2027."** ⚠️ **Two reads, both worth carrying. (a) For the memory makers, this is ~+80% ASP/GB on the flagship product with a second tier priced ~12% above it — the pricing power is real and it is being exercised differentially. (b) For the buyers, [[NVDA]] gets a ~10-15% BOM advantage over [[AVGO]]'s and [[AMD]]'s ASIC customers on the single most expensive non-logic component, which partly offsets the ASIC-TCO argument. Source variance to hold rather than average: the deck and group-call summary say $31-32/GB, while O'Mara's relay of the same analyst the same day says "$30-31 per GB next year, up from $20 before."** **On that BOM, Fubon models the Rubin GPU price at US$78-80K assuming NVDA holds a 75-80% chip gross margin.**
- **THE RUBIN HBM SUPPLY SEQUENCE, and it is not the order the market assumes: "only see SMALL VOLUME of [[MU]] and [[SAMSUNG]] HBM in supply chain"; "Rubin with [[SKHYNIX]]'s HBM4 will only START RAMPING IN LATE-AUG/SEP WITH BIGGER VOLUME." Shang restated it directly — "he has seen Samsung and Micron HBM related products for Nvidia BUT NOT HYNIX, that will come later in the year" (O'Mara headline: "HYNIX BEHIND SAMSUNG IN HBM").** ⚠️ **Samsung and Micron are qualified first but at small volume; Hynix is still the volume vendor, arriving ~a month out. Do not read the sequence as a share statement.** **Demand-side offset: [[NVDA]] Rubin FY26 volumes cut to 1mn from 1.5mn pushes some HBM4 demand right, but Blackwell absorbs the units ("net net neutral impact to NVDA") and RUBIN ULTRA HBM CONTENT IS UNCHANGED despite the 4-compute-die cancellation.**

**★ ③ THE NEW SUPPLY OVERHANG, AND IT IS LARGE: CXMT capacity to more than DOUBLE past 600kwpm (@jukan05 relaying Reuters, 2026-08-03, 415 likes).** **"Reuters reported that CHINA'S CXMT is considering building a SECOND DRAM FAB IN BEIJING's Yizhuang district, in addition to the new fabs currently under construction in SHANGHAI and HEFEI. Including the Hefei and Shanghai fabs now under construction, CXMT's TOTAL CAPACITY IS EXPECTED TO MORE THAN DOUBLE FROM CURRENT LEVELS TO OVER 600,000 WAFERS PER MONTH. The PROPOSED SECOND BEIJING FAB IS NOT INCLUDED IN THAT FIGURE."** ⚠️ **This is the structural argument against the up-cycle's DURATION rather than its current pricing, and it is the counterweight to every LTA-based bull argument on this page. A >600kwpm CXMT lands mostly at the commodity/legacy DRAM end, which is where the marginal supply matters least for HBM and most for the conventional-DRAM pricing that funds the HBM capex. Cross-file [themes/china-export](china-export.md).**

**④ DEMAND-SIDE CONFIRMATION FROM OUTSIDE THE MEMORY COMPLEX, and it is the largest consumer buyer in the world putting its own balance sheet behind it ([[AAPL]] 10-Q via Morgan Stanley · Erik Woodring, 2026-08-03).** **Apple's filing now warns of "SUPPLY CONSTRAINTS AND INCREASING COSTS FOR COMPONENTS" driven by advanced semiconductors and NAND/DRAM, and that it "EXPECTS THESE TRENDS TO INTENSIFY." The behaviour behind the language: on-balance-sheet inventory grew NEARLY 90% Y/Y exiting the June quarter, and including non-trade receivables and manufacturing purchase obligations, overall inventory rose 38% Y/Y — an all-time high for the quarter. Tim Cook has said Apple is "leveraging its own balance sheet to mitigate the impact from the memory crunch."** ⚠️ **MS's own caveat: "it's unclear how much of this inventory is memory." Also visible at the point of sale — MacBook Air shortages with orders slipping to September and Apple steering buyers to the entry MacBook Pro (@markgurman, 2026-08-02).** **And on the hyperscaler side, [[AMZN]] raised CY26 capex ~$200bn → ~$220bn with "MEMORY COST INFLATION AS THE CULPRIT" (MS Cloud Capex Tracker, 2026-08-03) — i.e. the buyer absorbed the inflation in the budget rather than in the spec, which is the same conclusion as ① from a different direction.**

**⑤ Equipment-layer read on NAND, which has been the weaker leg of the memory thesis: [[TER]]'s IR told a UBS-hosted call that memory test "has STRENGTHENED and NOT DOWN IN THE 2H, OPPOSITE — DRAM driven; NAND STRENGTHENING AS WELL," and expects "single-digit share gains not only [in] HBM, but NAND as well" (UBS · Robert Ruple, 2026-08-03). [[LRCX]] independently cites "STRONGER-THAN-EXPECTED NAND CAPEX" as a 2027 growth lever (MS · Shane Brett via Lee Simpson, 2026-08-03), and MS flags [[SNDK]]'s Sept-Q EPS guide as a "VERY LIKELY UPSIDE SURPRISE" — the highest confidence tier in its Week-3 table (MS · Joseph Moore, 2026-08-03).** ⚠️ **Three independent points in the NAND chain — test, capex, and the merchant — pointing the same way inside 24 hours. Worth weighting more than any one of them.**

**★ ⑥ THE PRIMARY TRANSCRIPT BEHIND ① AND ②, AND IT CHANGES TWO OF THE CLAIMS ABOVE (Fubon · Sherman Shang / Arthur (hosted by Jefferies), "industry highlights & checks update", 2026-08-03 — machine transcript; ASR garbles names: `Fubon`←"Foobon", `Blackwell`←"Black Wheel"/"Blackwall", `CoWoS`←"CoOS", `Titan`←"Tycan").** Four things the relays did not carry:
- **⚠️ CORRECTION TO ② — THE $/GB YEAR IS AMBIGUOUS IN THE SOURCE ITSELF, and this page should stop dating it.** The same transcript says **"Nvidia's HBM4 cost will increase from 17 to $18 per gigabyte to 30 to 32 per gigabyte IN 2026"** in the presentation and, in Q&A on the same figure, **"the HBM cost IN 2027 will increase to around 30 to 32 per gigabyte."** ⚠️ **Both quoted; neither adopted. The magnitude — a ~1.8x step-up in HBM $/GB — is the robust part, and it is what drives the Rubin BOM. Hold this alongside the $31-32/GB relay mark and O'Mara's "$30-31 next year, up from $20 before" as three renderings of one check rather than three datapoints.**
- **⚠️ NUANCE TO ② — "RUBIN ULTRA HBM CONTENT IS UNCHANGED" IS TRUE OF THE SPEC AND FALSE OF THE SHIPPING SKU, and the difference is the whole content debate.** Fubon, verbatim: **"in the short term, we haven't seen Nvidia changing the HBM content in Rubin Ultra. We only see the four compute die is now canceled, while the TWO COMPUTE DIE RUBIN ULTRA CHIP HAS LESS HBM CONTENT."** The numbers, with the Blackwell reference points he gave in the same Q&A:

| Part | Compute dies | HBM per package | HBM generation / $ per GB |
|---|---|---|---|
| B200 | — | **~192 GB** | HBM3-class, **$17-18/GB** |
| B300 | — | **~288 GB** | HBM3-class, **$17-18/GB** |
| Rubin Ultra (original) | **4** | **">1,024 GB per chip"** | HBM4, **$30-32/GB** — **SKU CANCELLED** |
| Rubin Ultra (surviving) | **2** | **"around 384 GB"** | HBM4, **$30-32/GB** |

  ⚠️ **So the top-of-stack SKU that would have carried >1TB of HBM per package no longer exists, and the part that replaces it carries roughly a third of that. Nvidia did not de-spec Rubin Ultra; it deleted the high-content variant. For the bit-demand model that is the same outcome by a different route, and it is a genuine content headwind to size against the price step-up — which is why this page should keep "no de-spec" (①) and "less content" (here) as two separate, both-true findings rather than netting them.** ★ **Corroboration from the supplier side: [[SAMSUNG]] is sampling 12-Hi (48GB) HBM4E rather than 16-Hi (Arete RTV #68, 2026-07-21), and 8 × 48GB = 384GB — a sampling decision and an ODM content check independently arriving at the same number.**
- **★ THE PUSH-OUT HAS AN HBM CAUSE, WHICH MAKES THE MEMORY LAYER A SOURCE OF THE 2H26 SLIP AND NOT ONLY A CASUALTY OF IT: "due to the thermal design change AND THE HBM BASE DIE ISSUE causing the production push out by AROUND TWO MONTHS," with Rubin volumes cut to ~1mn units in 2H26 — 200-300K in Q3 stepping to 700-800K in Q4.** ⚠️ **The base die is the one HBM4 component the pure-memory makers do not build (TSMC N12/N3 for [[MU]] and [[SKHYNIX]]; only [[SAMSUNG]] does it in-house), so this is the first execution problem in the up-cycle that sits inside the HBM stack rather than at the accelerator vendor. It is also the mechanism behind the supply-chain guide cut logged on [outros-asia](outros-asia.md) (KYEC 2026 revenue guide "up low-40s%" → "high-30s%").** **And the supplier sequence that paces the recovery: "we currently only see a SMALL VOLUME OF MICRON AND SAMSUNG'S HBM. And the Rubin with Hynix HBM4 will START TO RAMP UP AT LATE AUGUST OR SEPTEMBER WITH A BIGGER VOLUME, which is why in Q4 we will see a higher volume of Rubin" — i.e. SK hynix is the volume HBM4 vendor into Rubin, Micron and Samsung are qualified but small. Read as qualification-and-volume, not as share ([[SKHYNIX]] · [[MU]] · [[SAMSUNG]]).**
- **★ THE DE-SPEC REBUTTAL IN THE ODMs' OWN WORDS, WITH THE 2027 SPEC SHEETS AND THE BUDGET BEHIND IT (Arthur, from Taiwan ODM visits "last two week").** **"They talking about downside memory module to reduce the cost was discussion. But ODM say there will be very serious impact the AI server performance. And… currently demand are unfeasible for AI server."** Then the forward evidence: **"especially the big ODM like Hon Hai and 'Weichuang' [ASR-garbled Taiwanese ODM] looking for 2027 — when they see the spec, they don't see any the this spec for memory… including the Google AWS, they don't see any spec change for 2027,"** and **"for their purchasing team they say they are rising the capex for buy and sell for the GPU."** The BOM the budgets are built on: **"for the GB200 per rack is running 3 million or 3.5 million. For Rubin, there will be 7 million to 8 million,"** with Fubon deriving a **$78-80K Rubin GPU price off an assumed 75-80% NVDA chip gross margin.** ⚠️ **This is the strongest form of ① available: not a denial from a memory seller, but three separate procurement-side artefacts — the engineering objection, the 2027 spec sheets for [[GOOG]] and [[AMZN]] platforms, and a raised capex budget. The bear needs a spec change AND a budget cut; neither is visible at the layer that would execute both.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_08_03_sherman_update_03_08_26.html)

**⑦ THE NUMBERS UNDER ④, BECAUSE THE MAGNITUDE IS THE POINT ([[AAPL]] 10-Q via Morgan Stanley · Erik Woodring, "What's New in the 10-Q, Including 2 Post-Earnings Thoughts", 2026-08-03).** **On-balance-sheet inventory **+87% y/y to $11B exiting the June quarter, an all-time high — ~18 DAYS of inventory, the highest since the March '18 quarter**. Including off-balance-sheet items (vendor non-trade receivables + manufacturing purchase obligations), **~$96bn, +38% y/y, an all-time high for a June quarter**; B/S inventory + vendor non-trade receivables alone **+53% y/y and +29% q/q**. MS's read: **"this reflects NON-SoC INVENTORY PURCHASES to ensure ample supply ex-bottlenecks, and it's unclear how much of this inventory is memory."** The filing's own language is the more durable datapoint: **"supply constraints and increasing costs for components"** driven by advanced semiconductors and **NAND/DRAM**, that Apple **"expects these trends to intensify,"** and — the concession that matters for the pass-through debate — that **"actions, such as price increases, that have been and may in the future be taken by the Company MAY NOT EFFECTIVELY MITIGATE these negative impacts"** and could reduce demand. ⚠️ **Why it belongs on this page rather than only on the Apple page: this is the world's largest consumer electronics buyer HOARDING AHEAD OF EXPECTED FURTHER TIGHTENING, using its own balance sheet, and saying in a filing that it may not be able to price its way out. Consumer/mobile has been the soft leg of the memory bull case all quarter (allocation-driven, per [[SKHYNIX]] and [[SAMSUNG]] management); a pre-buy of this size says the buyer expects 2027 to be worse than 2026, not better. It is also the cleanest available answer to the "who pays" question: the buyer is paying and pre-paying.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/APPLE_20260803_1311.html)

**★ ⑧ THE MECHANISM THAT MAKES HBM *CAPACITY* — NOT BANDWIDTH — THE BINDING CONSTRAINT ON AGENTIC SERVING, AND IT MAKES SERVER DRAM A COMPLEMENT RATHER THAN A SUBSTITUTE (SemiAnalysis · Kimbo Chen / Shubham Choudhari / Bryan Shan / Dylan Patel, "Kimi K3, The Manos, The Mythos, The Legendos", 2026-08-03).** **The hierarchy, stated plainly: "KV cache resides in HBM, the fastest memory in a GPU cluster, CONSUMING WHATEVER CAPACITY IS LEFT BY MODEL WEIGHTS AND ACTIVATIONS. As KV cache size exceeds the HBM capacity, IT SPILLS INTO SERVER DRAM, a higher capacity but lower bandwidth memory pool. Finally, when KV cache exceeds DRAM capacity, it spills to disk storage such as SSD."** The measured consequence on a real serving stack: **on a B300 node running Kimi K3, "after accounting for the weights, GPU HBM CAN ONLY HOLD 3.25M TOK" of KV cache; throughput rises with batch size only until CONCURRENCY EXCEEDS 8, at which point "cache starts to thrash, resulting in HIT RATES FALLING TO <10% WHEN THE THEORETICAL HIT RATE IS 95%." Add DRAM: "for a system with 219.91 GB/RANK CPU DRAM OFFLOAD, this results in an ADDITIONAL 15.76M TOKENS worth of KV cache, or a 4.85× TOTAL… and the performance gain is huge," enabling concurrency above 8 without thrashing. On the prior generation: "on B200 this is MUCH WORSE AND NEARLY INFEASIBLE."** ⚠️ **Three read-throughs, all of which this page has been arguing qualitatively and can now argue mechanically. (a) HBM capacity per package is a REVENUE-CAPACITY variable for the operator, not a spec-sheet number — the difference between concurrency 8 and concurrency 30+ on the same node is the difference between two utilisation regimes, which is why ODMs call a memory-module cut "unfeasible for AI server" in ⑥ rather than merely undesirable. (b) SERVER DRAM IS ADDITIVE, NOT ALTERNATIVE: 219.91 GB/rank of CPU DRAM buys 4.85x the KV budget, so agentic serving pulls conventional DRAM content per AI server UP at the same time as it pulls HBM — the "commodity DRAM is the funding leg, HBM is the growth leg" framing on this page understates the demand link. (c) It dates the obsolescence risk in the installed base: the same workload is "nearly infeasible" one generation back, which is a capacity-retirement argument for total memory demand rather than a like-for-like replacement. Cross-file [tokenmaxxing](tokenmaxxing.md) for the token-economics half of the same note.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Semianalysis_on_K3.html)

## Recent signals (Aug 2) — ★ [[SAMSUNG]] PUTS NUMBERS ON THE LTA ASYMMETRY (−5% floor vs +10-20% open, 60-70% of sales, 5-year ROLLING), the July contract print splits DRAM from NAND, and the buyers describe the option to stop paying

**★ ① THE LTA TERMS ARE NOW QUANTIFIED, AND THEY ARE AN ASYMMETRIC OPTION RATHER THAN A SUPPLY CONTRACT (BofA · Simon Woo, "Simon's tech check (2 Aug)", 2026-08-02).** **"Samsung Electronics (SEC) said 60-70% OF ITS MEMORY SALES WILL BE UNDER LTA (long-term agreement) but WITH FAVORABLE TERMS (PRICE CUT LIMIT vs BROADLY OPEN FOR PRICE INCREASE; e.g., SUB-5% DOWN QoQ vs 10-20%+ INCREASE). Interestingly, SEC's LTA (MAINLY FOR US BIG TECH) is mostly for 5 YEARS ROLLING; thus, THE 6TH YEAR CAN BE ADDED RIGHT AFTER/BEFORE 1ST YEAR COMPLETION."**
- ⚠️ **THIS SUPERSEDES THE JUL 31 "LTA MECHANICS" ENTRY ON THIS PAGE BY MAKING IT SPECIFIC, AND IT IS THE MOST IMPORTANT STRUCTURAL FACT IN THE MEMORY COMPLEX RIGHT NOW. On roughly two-thirds of the largest DRAM producer's book, the DOWNSIDE IS CAPPED AT −5% PER QUARTER WHILE THE UPSIDE IS UNCAPPED AT +10-20%+. Two consequences follow directly. (a) THE NEXT DOWNTURN HITS VOLUMES BEFORE PRICES — a producer contractually protected on price will defend price and let utilisation fall, which changes the shape of every historical memory-cycle analogue this wiki has used. (b) THE "RISING LTA COVERAGE = LOWER SPOT UPSIDE" READ IS WRONG AS APPLIED HERE; coverage this asymmetric is a floor, not a cap.**
- **The 5-year ROLLING structure is the second under-appreciated detail: a book that adds a sixth year as the first completes never runs down to a contractual cliff. It is closer to an evergreen than to a backlog, which means "contracted through 20XX" statements from memory makers should not be read the way order-book statements from equipment makers are.**
- ⚠️ **THE OPEN QUESTION THIS CREATES, and it is the highest-value disclosure gap on this theme: [[SKHYNIX]] and [[MU]] do not disclose LTA coverage or terms at anything like this granularity. If Samsung secured a −5%/quarter floor across two-thirds of its book with US hyperscalers as counterparties, the relevant question for the other two is whether they MATCHED those terms or CONCEDED PRICE PROTECTION TO WIN VOLUME. Nothing on this wiki answers it. Raise on the next calls / via IR — it is more decision-relevant than another quarter of spot prints.**
- **Adjacent Korea BOM datapoint from the same note, relevant to AI-server content and to the supplier-financing pattern: SEMCO and LG Innotek both guided "VERY BULLISH" on AI-server MLCC and FC-BGA at their 2Q calls — "(1) STRONGER ORDER UNDER LTA; (2) LARGE AMOUNT OF PREPAYMENTS (key financial resources for new fab construction or capacity expansion); and (3) CONTRACT PRICE INCREASE THROUGH 2H AND 2027," with 2Q OP MORE THAN DOUBLING Y/Y "even with MID-CYCLE LEVEL of MLCC/FC-BGA margin," and new Vietnam FC-BGA fab operations from mid-to-late 2027.** ⚠️ **Customer PREPAYMENTS funding supplier capex is now visible across the chain ([[ORCL]]'s ~$75bn of prepaid hardware contracts, vendor-financed neoclouds, and here) — the AI build is increasingly being financed by its customers rather than by its suppliers' balance sheets. Worth tracking as a category. See [themes/hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md).**

**★ ② THE JULY CONTRACT PRINT: DRAM +30-50% Q/Q, NAND FLAT — the cleanest divergence of the cycle.** **"Newly confirmed JULY CONTRACT PRICE INCREASE (TrendForce's updated July DRAM contract price: UP LOW-TEEN% MoM OR 30-50% QoQ but FLAT FOR NAND)," with spot expected to rally further into August on three named drivers: "(1) chip order increase for INVENTORY RESTOCKING; (2) many TIER-2 OEMs RESUMING new smartphone/PC model launch TARGETING SEPT/4Q DESPITE HIGH MEMORY COST; and (3) BOTTOMING OUT CHINA DOMESTIC SMARTPHONE SHIPMENT (only 17MN UNITS IN JUNE vs normal 25-30MN; LEAN CHANNEL INVENTORIES)."**
- ⚠️ **DRIVER (2) IS THE ONE TO STRESS-TEST BECAUSE IT IS ALSO THE BEAR CASE. Tier-2 OEMs building INTO high memory cost is either genuine inelastic demand or a pull-forward ahead of further increases. [[SNDK]]'s own preview the same day describes precisely the latter destroying bit growth: Edge was 62% of March revenue and grew "NEARLY 120% Q/Q AS CUSTOMERS RACED TO BUILD INVENTORY AHEAD OF ANTICIPATED PRICE INCREASES. At a certain point, the Edge has built enough inventory and THAT REVENUE WILL BEGIN TO FALL OFF" (Jefferies · Curtis, 2026-08-02). And [[MEDIATEK]]'s covering analyst attributes part of a −13%/−21%/−22% cut to 2026-28 smartphone units to 1H26 pull-in "on the expectation of a continued rise in memory prices" (JPM, 2026-08-02). Same behaviour, three pages, opposite conclusions depending on which side of the pull-forward you sit.**
- **NAND FLAT is the operative fact for [[SNDK]]/[[KIOXIA]]: the ASP-extrapolation trade that carried those names is not being validated by the July contract print, and the analyst who was "bullish NAND vs DRAM given the concentration from Memory vendors on prioritizing DRAM expansion vs. NAND" now says "THIS TREND MAY BE SHIFTING" (Jefferies, 2026-08-02). ⚠️ Note the equipment layer is spending into NAND anyway — [[LRCX]] "NAND revenue MORE THAN DOUBLING Q/Q" this week — i.e. capacity commitment is running ahead of NAND pricing, the normal but risk-bearing sequence.**
- **On the China supply threat, Woo is dismissive and supplies the mechanism, which is more useful than the conclusion: "we see MINIMAL IMPACT from China local chipmakers' capacity expansion — CHINA-BRANDED MEMORY NOT COMMONLY TRADED IN THE SPOT MARKET; even Korea/US/Japan chips NOT MUCH AVAILABLE FOR WHITE-BOX OEMs."** ⚠️ **Two offsetting facts in the same week: CXMT's IPO printed +500% ON DAY ONE (Chris Wood / GREED & fear, via Global SEMIS Daily, 2026-08-02), so the China memory-capex FUNDING constraint just loosened materially; and press reports have CXMT, [[SMIC]] and Hua Hong ordering 25 IMMERSION LITHO SYSTEMS FROM A DOMESTIC SUPPLIER (see [[ASML]]). Neither changes 2026 bits; both change the 2028 supply curve. Also logged: Roundhill's DRAM ETF took CXMT to 2.47% weight by selling ~3mn Samsung shares (@The_AI_Investor, 08-01) — a mechanical, non-fundamental seller of Samsung that will recur at each rebalance.**

**★ ③ THE DEMAND-SIDE RISK, NOW STATED BY THE BUYERS THEMSELVES — and the offsetting evidence that they are currently paying.** ⚠️ **[[MSFT]] CFO Amy Hood: capex has "really pivot[ed] toward… SHORT-LIVED ASSETS, which really, that's CPUs and GPUs… IF THE DEMAND ENVIRONMENT CHANGES, YOU JUST SLOW DOWN WHAT IS, IN FACT, THE LARGEST COMPONENT AND THE DRIVER OF COGS," and "when you have the ability to LATE BIND some of the more expensive components in short term, you have a big book of business that's FLEXIBLE." [[AMZN]]'s Jassy: "IF THE DEMAND ISN'T THERE, WE WON'T SPEND THE CAPITAL." [[META]]'s CFO left 2028 open while "DEFERRING CHIPS." Culpan's application to this theme: such stop-starts are "bad for memory makers like Micron and SK Hynix because they are COMMODITY PLAYERS THAT SUFFER WILD PRICE SWINGS WHICH CAN TURN PROFIT TO LOSS IN THE BLINK OF AN EYE" (Culpium, 2026-08-02).**
- **THE COUNTERWEIGHT IS CURRENTLY STRONGER AND IS ALSO NEW THIS WEEK: BofA's Vivek Arya has cloud capex "approaching $860BN IN CY26 and $1.2TN IN CY27… now backed by $2.3TN+ OF CUSTOMER COMMITMENT," names MEMORY as one of five key beneficiary buckets, and states that "hyperscalers ALSO POINT OUT THEIR CAPEX OUTLOOK INCORPORATES RISING INPUT COSTS (MEMORY, WAFERS, SUBSTRATES), i.e. SEMIS PRICING POWER." Morgan Stanley's supply-chain team, independently: the capex raises show hyperscalers "RAISING SPENDING TO ABSORB THOSE COSTS RATHER THAN CUTTING UNIT VOLUMES. That should ease investor concerns that higher component prices would REDUCE OVERALL SERVER UNIT PROCUREMENT" (MS · Howard Kao, 2026-08-02). AWS's own $20bn capex raise was explicitly attributed to higher memory costs.**
- ⚠️ **NET, AND THIS IS THE STATE OF THE DEBATE: the hyperscalers are paying the memory inflation, saying so, and building it into guidance — while simultaneously describing the mechanism by which they could stop. Both facts are new. The arbitrating observable is capex MIX (short- vs long-lived assets) rather than headline capex, plus GPU-install cadence vs shell-build cadence over 2-3 quarters. ⚠️ Add GS's macro warning as a third input: "official US data imply that recent increases in AI investment have INCREASINGLY BEEN DRIVEN BY COST INFLATION, suggesting that spending increases in 2026 may provide a SMALLER BOOST TO REAL INVESTMENT than in 2025" (GS Global Economics, 2026-08-02) — nominal capex growth is overstating BIT growth, and the memory complex is the reason why.**
- **Pass-through evidence reaching end prices, which is the cleanest confirmation that this is inflation rather than margin transfer: [[AMD]] is raising bundled GPU-die + VRAM prices to board partners by ~10% on the memory shortage, having delayed the increase since June (@QQ_Timmy, 2026-08-02); [[AAPL]] guided FQ4 below on component constraints with margin weakness ex-tariffs; and the whole smartphone chain ([[QCOM]], [[ARM]], Skyworks, STM) flagged H2-26 challenges "DUE TO THE HEADWIND FROM RISING MEMORY PRICES."**

## Recent signals (Aug 1, PRIMARY SOURCES) — the equipment layer's read on memory, from two calls the wiki had been reading second-hand
_⚠️ Recovery ingest: the [[KLAC]] (2026-07-28) and [[TER]] (2026-07-29) transcripts were archived unrouted on 07-31 and restored on 08-01. Both are equipment vendors, so this is the **capacity-provisioning** view of the memory cycle — a leading indicator of bit supply, not of pricing._
- **★ THE NAND INFLECTION HAS A DATE, AND IT IS RECENT.** Teradyne's CEO, on what changed since January: *"In January… we anticipated that we would see a strengthening in NAND, but **we didn't hear that from our memory customers yet**… what's different now is we're actually have **seen the beginning of that inflection and are actually getting pull from our customers for more NAND capacity**."* ⚠️ **This is the test layer confirming the NAND turn from order flow rather than from price prints — memory makers do not buy final-test capacity for bits they do not intend to ship.** Corroborated on the [[KLAC]] side, where management expects *"some **greenfield investment in flash**"* in 2027 — ⚠️ **which directly contests the carried JPM view that the first NAND greenfield does not arrive until CY28** (Teradyne Q2 CY26 call, 2026-07-29; KLA Q4 FY26 call, 2026-07-28).
- **DDR — not HBM — was the upside surprise of the quarter, and the attribution is agentic AI.** *"The thing that surprised us a little bit was **just how strong the DDR business is**… that is, to a certain extent coupled to the resurgence in like CPU-oriented applications, so **agentic AI**. There's also… **SOCAMMs use LPDDRs** that are driving that market harder than we would have expected. So **the DDR market is stronger now than we thought it would be in January**."* HBM was expected strong (2024 big, 2025 digestion, 2026 strong off long-term capacity plans) — the delta is conventional/server DRAM (Teradyne Q2 CY26 call, 2026-07-29).
- **Sizing from the test layer: the 2026 memory test TAM is guided *"more than 40% larger than 2025,"* with notable 1H→2H growth**, and TER's own memory revenue hit a record **$212M** — a third straight quarter above $200M — at a **book-to-bill over 2x**. ⚠️ **A >2x book-to-bill in memory test is a capacity signal for 2027 bit supply**: *"memory manufacturers are planning capacity additions further out in time."* Into 2027, *"I don't think we are looking at particular lumpiness in memory… there are fabs that are coming online… they are all making their test capacity add plans out into 2027 now"* (Teradyne Q2 CY26 call, 2026-07-29).
- **⚠️ THE UNRESOLVED BEAR ARITHMETIC WAS PUT TO MANAGEMENT LIVE AND PARTIALLY DEFLECTED — worth carrying, because it is this theme's central question.** MS's Shane Brett asked how memory test can grow ~2x bit growth when TER's own deck shows memory test time growing at roughly *half* the rate of bit growth. Greg Smith: ***"I think I'm going to need to take that question offline. You're interpreting a lot of information from that chart that I'm not sure we were intending to communicate."*** The structural answer he did give: memory test has **two flywheels** — bit growth (served by *"much lower priced, less differentiated equipment"*) and **technology transitions** (HBM3→HBM4, DDR5→DDR6, next-gen flash) — and *"the SOC TAM is 5x the size of the memory TAM, even though the memory revenues… have caught up because of **ASP changes**."* **So the claim is that the gap between ~62% y/y memory test revenue and ~30% bit growth is technology-transition mix plus ASP, not overshoot. Asserted, not evidenced** (Teradyne Q2 CY26 call, 2026-07-29).
- **From the process-control side: memory intensity is now *"rivaling"* advanced logic on some products — but HBM and conventional DRAM must not be conflated.** KLA: *"we're seeing even across some of our products where we're seeing **intensity levels that are rivaling what we see in advanced logic**"* — while explicitly separating the two, *"**conventional DRAM isn't the same as high bandwidth memory DRAM in terms of its process control intensity**."* The 2027 driver is **greenfield DRAM fabs** rather than tech upgrades, and the economic argument is pricing-driven: *"given whether it's HBM or even conventional DRAM, you can imagine that where prices are, how that translates into margins, **the value of yield is pretty high**."* Sept-quarter mix guided to **memory ~27% of KLA's semi-PC systems to semiconductor customers (DRAM ~90% / NAND ~10%)**, ⚠️ **with memory expected to be a bigger share in the December quarter "and likely to remain there through the first half"** of CY27 (KLA Q4 FY26 call, 2026-07-28).
- **⚠️ The memory-cost pass-through is now hurting the equipment vendors themselves, and it is bigger and longer than previously logged.** KLA: *"we've been having to **procure a new memory at higher prices**… we thought the impact was somewhere around **100 basis points. It's probably a little bit more than that. I think that likely continues as we move forward through 2027**"* — before normalising from headwind to tailwind. **The memory shortage is no longer only a cost line for OEMs and hyperscalers; it is compressing the gross margins of the tools that build memory** (KLA Q4 FY26 call, 2026-07-28).

## Recent signals (Aug 1) — the NAND side splits three ways on QLC, and it is a genuine strategy divergence rather than a timing difference
- **★ THE SET-UP: with [[SKHYNIX]] (07-28), [[SAMSUNG]] (07-29) and [[KIOXIA]] (07-31) all printed, "the QLC SUPPLY RAMP APPEARS TO BE SHIFTING SLIGHTLY TO THE RIGHT, though it remains on track to ACCELERATE IN 2H26 as suppliers migrate to ultra-high-density nodes"** (Susquehanna · Mehdi Hosseini, Tech Hardware weekly, 2026-08-01). **The right-shift is near-term supportive of NAND pricing; the 2H26 acceleration is the medium-term risk. But the more useful finding is that the three suppliers are NOT doing the same thing:**
  - **[[SAMSUNG]] — the aggressor. "Expects QLC BIT SHIPMENTS IN 2H26 TO MORE THAN DOUBLE VS 1H26, supported by its newly developed V9 2Tb QLC and the SCHEDULED MASS PRODUCTION OF V10 V-NAND IN AUGUST 2026 to address undersupplied AI infrastructure."** ⚠️ **An August-2026 V10 MP date is the hardest, nearest-dated NAND roadmap milestone in this theme — near-term falsifiable, and worth checking at the next quarterly.**
  - **[[KIOXIA]] — explicitly declining to chase it. "Continues to PRIORITIZE TLC eSSDs for performance-critical inferencing, leveraging its 8th-gen BiCS FLASH, as HIGH-CAPACITY QLC SOLUTIONS REMAIN TOO COSTLY for broad deployment."** Separately, **Kioxia has begun shipping SAMPLES OF 9TH-GENERATION FLASH** (Nikkei Asia, same weekly).
  - **[[SKHYNIX]] — node migration rather than cell-level bet. "Targeting its 321-LAYER technology to reach 50% OF DOMESTIC PRODUCTION BY YEAR-END to support surging eSSD demand."**
- ⚠️ **WHY THE DIVERGENCE MATTERS MORE THAN THE RAMP: these are opposite readings of the same customer requirement. Samsung is betting AI infrastructure needs cheap density NOW; Kioxia is betting inference needs TLC PERFORMANCE and that QLC's cost per useful IOP does not clear. One of them is mis-allocating capital, and the answer shows up in 2027 NAND mix and pricing. The read-through for [[SNDK]] is direct and under-appreciated — it shares fabs with Kioxia, so its QLC positioning is gated by a partner that has publicly decided QLC is too costly. That is a question for the 08-05 SNDK call, not a settled fact.**
- **Demand-side timing from the controller layer: SIMO expects QLC to begin ramping toward END-2026 through FIVE TIER-1 CSP ENGAGEMENTS for its MonTitan platform, "with adoption becoming more meaningful in 2027-28 as 2Tb dies become more widely available and SSDs REACH THE DENSITY CROSSOVER POINT NEEDED TO DISPLACE TRADITIONAL HDDs."** ⚠️ **THIS DIRECTLY CONTRADICTS THE HDD SIDE'S OWN FRAMING PUBLISHED THE SAME DAY, and the contradiction should be carried unresolved rather than netted: the [[STX]]/[[WDC]] read has enterprise SSD confined to high-performance inference (KV-cache), "the PACE OF SSD REPLACING NEARLINE HDD SLOWING," rising NAND prices WIDENING the HDD cost advantage, and HDD remaining the cold-data workhorse "over the next five years" (@QQ_Timmy, 2026-08-01). Two industries, opposite claims about the same substitution, with the NAND side dating the crossover to 2027-28 and the HDD side pushing it past 2030. The 2Tb-die availability schedule is the observable that arbitrates it.**
- **Cost pass-through continues to show up outside the memory complex — three fresh instances from the same weekly's link flow, all press-relayed rather than company-sourced: "[[NVDA]] & [[AMD]] graphics card prices see massive increase in China as GDDR6 & GDDR7 costs continue to climb, & shops begin STOCKPILING GPUs"; "[[NVDA]] TRIMS VERA RUBIN MEMORY as HBM4 prices threaten to eat 29% OF EVERY RACK'S COST"; and "MacBook Ultra's adoption to suffer due to insane OLED premium AND DRAM PRICES, leaving [[AAPL]] to stick with just one supplier for now."** ⚠️ **The 29%-of-rack-cost figure is the one to test — if HBM4 really approaches ~29% of rack BOM it is the single largest line item and it makes memory the gating input on rack economics rather than a component. Sourced to a wccftech report, not to NVIDIA or a primary supply-chain check; NOT adopted, flagged for verification.** **Also logged, on the China axis: "CXMT is betting on CUSTOMIZED DRAM to circumvent U.S. sanctions on HBM; Korean suppliers playing safe could miss out"; "CXMT EVICTED HUAWEI-LINKED ENGINEERS from its R&D facility"; "[[SKHYNIX]] plans to diminish CXMT's golden period with… technological superiority, as it wins over Chinese customers with LPDDR6"; "Innosilicon's LPDDR6 memory IP lands at a major Korean DRAM maker"; and "[[MU]] CEO Sanjay Mehrotra's bad blood with [[AAPL]] traces back years… amid chip standoff & efforts to procure from China's CXMT."** ⚠️ **All press relays in a link roundup — logged as the shape of the China-memory narrative this week, none adopted as fact.** (Susquehanna · Mehdi Hosseini, 2026-08-01)

## Recent signals (Jul 31) — ★ the LTA mechanics are disclosed, and they invert how rising contract coverage should be read
- **★ THE FINDING THAT MATTERS MOST, because it overturns the standard late-cycle reading of high contract coverage: "LTA DOES NOT FIX MEMORY CHIP PRICE… lot of room to RAISE contract price every quarter but there is LIMIT FOR PRICE CUT." Specifically — [[SAMSUNG]] said 60-70% OF ITS MEMORY SALES WILL BE UNDER LTA, but with ASYMMETRIC terms: a price-cut FLOOR of SUB-5% DOWN Q/Q against price INCREASES BROADLY OPEN AT 10-20%+. And on tenor: Samsung's LTAs (mainly for US Big Tech) are mostly 5-YEAR ROLLING, so a 6th year can be added right after or before the 1st year completes** (BofA · Simon Woo / Dai Shen / Vivek Arya, Global Memory Tech weekly, 2026-07-31).
- **⚠️ THE IMPLICATION, stated plainly because it is easy to get backwards: these are DOWNSIDE COLLARS WITH UNCAPPED UPSIDE on a rolling tenor — not price locks. High LTA coverage is therefore BULLISH for realised ASP stability rather than a cap on it. Two further consequences: (a) SPOT PRINTS BECOME A PROGRESSIVELY WEAKER SIGNAL of realised pricing as coverage rises; and (b) the usual bear argument that "LTAs will lock in today's prices before the cycle rolls" does not apply on these terms.**
- **READ-THROUGH ONLY (the source is an [[ASML]] Best-Idea note, not a memory note) — but it supplies the cleanest published estimate of what the DRAM buildout costs in LITHO terms, which this theme tracks only qualitatively** (Bernstein · Dai/Milano/Hwang "ASML: Best Idea 3Q26", 2026-07-30) [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/BERN_258544.html). **The equipment-side confirmation of the DRAM shortage: ASML guided FY26 MEMORY system sales +75% Y/Y against +25% for advanced logic/foundry, and memory's share of ASML system sales has already moved from ~30% historically to ~50% in the last two quarters** — an independent, tool-vendor read that the capacity adds behind the HBM/DDR shortage are real and being ordered, not merely announced. Management's characterisation of the DRAM setup was **a "perfect storm"** — node migration *and* volume expansion at once, with customers "adding meaningful capacity today while also planning multiple future fab expansions," and **the HBM buildout named as the volume component on top of the technology migration.** **The cost-per-node arithmetic, which is the reusable part: DRAM litho intensity runs <20% at 1Y/1Z → 26% at 1c → ~30% at 1d, on EUV exposures per node of ~2 at 1a → 3 at 1b → 5 at 1c → 8 at 1d (low teens at 0b/0c); total DRAM EUV exposures compound 4.5x from 3.4 MWPM (2025) to 26.3 MWPM (2030), taking DRAM from 26% → 44% of all EUV exposures.** ⚠️ **Read from the memory makers' side rather than ASML's, that is a rising capital-intensity-per-bit headwind sitting underneath the current margin euphoria — and the note models EUV ASPs +~10% in 2027 and +7% in 2028 before any like-for-like hikes, so the tool bill inflates on top of the layer count.** Partial offset: HNA single-exposure replaces LNA multi-patterning, with **litho capex/opex ~1.2x but non-litho cost declining**, and DRAM gets better HNA economics than logic because **"DRAM dies are smaller than logic dies and therefore do not require stitching."** Adoption starts with **[[SKHYNIX]] (EXE:5200B assembled at M16, announced Sept 2025) and [[SAMSUNG]] (two EXE:5200B ordered, per industry reports) at the 1d node.** ⚠️ **One contested claim worth logging because it cuts against a standing bear argument: Bernstein reads the 6F²→4F² transition (around 0a) as "a TEMPORARY EASING of scaling requirements rather than a departure from the long-term trend" of shrinking feature sizes — i.e. NOT the capex air-pocket the 4F² bear case implies.**
- **LTA coverage is now disclosed across the complex, and it is high: SAMSUNG at 60-70% of memory sales; [[KIOXIA]] "on track toward our 50% LTA VOLUME COVERAGE FOR CY28 with key customers, providing long-term revenue visibility"** (Kioxia Jun-Q, 2026-07-31). **Price formation in NAND/DRAM now runs largely through contracts rather than spot.**
- **The pricing evidence, and it is emphatic — with volume restraint as the deliberate mechanism: KIOXIA's Jun-Q ASP was +70% Q/Q LIKE-FOR-LIKE on only LOW-SINGLE-DIGIT BIT GROWTH (OPM 75%, GM 78.1%). SAMSUNG posted DRAM OPM ~80% and NAND OPM ~70% in 2Q even after special bonuses, with DRAM ASP guided +23% Q/Q IN 3Q and +8% IN 4Q — after +91%/+45% in 1Q/2Q.** Samsung's 2Q OP was **W89.5TN**, and BofA models quarterly OP through **W100TN+ (annual W400TN+) in 3Q/4Q26.**
- **⚠️ BUT A CEILING ON ASP UPSIDE IS ALSO DISCLOSED, and it is a strategic choice rather than a market constraint: Samsung guides ASP FLAT-TO-SLIGHTLY-DOWN q/q through 2027-28 under LTAs and "chipmakers' STRATEGIC CONTROL (e.g., DECENT PRICE CUT PER AI SERVER CONTENT INCREASE)" — trading price for content growth. Annual OP still rises to W500TN+ on volume plus cost decline (yield). That is a share/volume strategy, not price maximisation, and it caps how far ASP can run even in shortage.** Capex W78-100tn p.a. 2026-28; FCF W200tn+ (2026) → W300tn+ (2027-28); 50% of FCF committed to shareholder returns.
- **⚠️ THE JULY CONTRACT PRINT SHOWS DRAM AND NAND DIVERGING — worth watching, because they have been moving together: TrendForce July DRAM contract price UP LOW-TEENS % M/M = 30-50% Q/Q, but FLAT FOR NAND.**
- **Spot sentiment positive into August on three drivers: (1) chip order increases for INVENTORY RESTOCKING; (2) many Tier-2 OEMs RESUMING smartphone/PC model launches for Sept/4Q DESPITE high memory cost; (3) China domestic smartphone shipments BOTTOMING OUT — only 17MN UNITS IN JUNE vs a normal 25-30mn, with lean channel inventories.**
- **On China supply, the mechanical reason it is not showing in spot: "minimal impact from China local chipmakers' capacity expansion — CHINA-BRANDED MEMORY NOT COMMONLY TRADED IN THE SPOT MARKET; even Korea/US/Japan chips not much available for white-box OEMs."**
- **Duration: SAMSUNG sees an "ONGOING DRAM SUPPLY SHORTAGE DUE TO AI, EVEN WITH NEW CAPACITY EXPANSION THROUGH 2027-2028," with more LTAs with Big Tech and an HBM4 ramp. KIOXIA guides CY26 bit growth high-teens % ("in line with Samsung guidance") and, for CY27, "DEMAND ANTICIPATED TO EXCEED SUPPLY." BofA: Korean results suggest "continued tight supply and elevated pricing AT LEAST THROUGH 2027."**
- **★ THE DEMAND-SIDE PROOF NOW SHOWS UP IN BUYERS' OWN NUMBERS, which is the strongest available evidence of pricing power: [[AMZN]] raised FY26 capex ~$200BN → ~$220BN with the $20BN INCREMENT ATTRIBUTED EXPLICITLY TO HIGHER MEMORY COSTS, and named "inflated prices right now on some of the components like memory and HARD DRIVES AND SSDs"; [[META]] had earlier attributed its higher 2026 capex primarily to component and memory pricing; [[AAPL]] guided Sept-Q gross margin down with MEMORY ACCOUNTING FOR >100% OF THE SEQUENTIAL COMPRESSION and said "for September, we expect to pay EVEN HIGHER memory costs" — with UBS modelling DRAM AND NAND ASPs +~30% Q/Q into the December quarter and AAPL Dec-Q GM down to 45.9%.** BofA's behavioural read: **"every hyperscaler has consistently flagged supply constraints, and is SEEMINGLY NOT BOTHERED BY RISING PRICES of chips/memory."**
- **⚠️ THE DEMAND MIX IS NARROW, and this is the main risk to the cycle: KIOXIA says agentic AI is "a primary growth catalyst" and traditional server NAND is also benefiting — but SMARTPHONE NAND DEMAND IS FLAT VS CY25 (units down for low-priced models, flat for high-priced) and PC NAND IS A SLIGHT DECLINE ON HIGHER BOM COSTS. The cycle is being carried entirely by datacenter; consumer is contributing nothing and is being actively rationed by price.** Corroborating: **[[MEDIATEK]] expects the handset market to DECLINE 15% in 2026**, with customers barbelling to flagship and entry and "optimiz[ing] customer cost structure with memory."
- **The WFE consequence, with a timing caveat: [[LRCX]] guided materially above expectations with 2027 likely a FOURTH CONSECUTIVE YEAR of WFE outgrowth on NAND upgrades and HBM-related DRAM investment; LRCX also expects DRAM to be the FASTEST-GROWING WFE segment in FY26. ⚠️ But NAND WFE is DOWN 1H'26 vs 1H'25 IN DOLLARS — the structural SLC/TLC-for-AI-memory-stack demand (more expensive, more capacity, "a must") is teed up for '28-'30, NOT 2026-27.** See [themes/semicap-wfe](semicap-wfe.md).
- **The passives/substrate layer is showing the SAME contract structure, which is corroborating evidence that the AI-server pull is broad rather than memory-specific: SEMCO and LG Innotek both guided bullishly on AI MLCC and FC-BGA, with 2Q OP MORE THAN DOUBLING Y/Y, stronger LTA orders, LARGE PREPAYMENTS funding new fab construction, and contract price increases through 2H and 2027. SEMCO annual OP already W2TN in 2026 (vs ~W1.0tn in 2022-25) and W5TN+ expected in 2027. All three top MLCC makers — SEMCO, Murata and Taiyo Yuden — have raised prices** (BofA · Woo, 2026-07-31; @jukan05, 2026-07-31).
- **Capital return arrives: KIOXIA announced a ¥800BN BUYBACK (up to 30mn shares = 5.5% of shares out, 3 Aug → 30 Oct 2026) plus a 3-FOR-1 STOCK SPLIT effective 1 Oct — funded from ¥187bn net cash and ¥827bn of post-capex FCF. SAMSUNG is "ready to announce a major buyback and large cash dividend using 50% of FCF," with a buyback framed as "a question of WHEN, NOT IF" on low treasury holdings; no immediate ADR plan.**
- **⚠️ Positioning, logged so the moves are not misread as fundamental: KOSPI +18% overnight with [[SKHYNIX]] +30% LIMIT UP and Samsung +27% as the Situational Awareness book cleared to Citadel. JPM: Korea's l/s ratio retraced to 2.8x (80th %-ile) from 6x with net exposures ~60% off highs — BUT STILL +4.5Z ABOVE THE 2018 AVERAGE. Crowding reduced, not resolved. Jefferies flags Korean retail selling single-stock leveraged products and "aggressively purchasing INVERSE leveraged products," warning that "concerns around leverage in the system and retail margin calls [have not] all gone away after a 1 day bounce." Korean ETF AUM ~W500TN across 1,000+ funds = 30%+ OF KOSPI TRADING VALUE.**

## Recent signals (Jul 27) — Morgan Stanley sizes the 2027 CONSUMER rationing, and calls memory inflation "a constraint on what gets built," not a transfer
- **★ THE 2027 CONSUMER SHORTFALL, QUANTIFIED IN UNITS (Morgan Stanley "Playing the AI Infrastructure Dip", Shawn Kim / Cindy Huang, 2026-07-27 — the AI-infrastructure note carrying the memory team's "Chipflation" framework).** Applying MS's memory-sufficiency framework separately to PCs and smartphones (assuming **PCs absorb 17% of non-server DRAM and smartphones 58%**), total addressable memory falls **~15% short for PCs and ~12% short for smartphones in 2027** — implying **unit downside of −58mn PCs (−15% vs MS's own shipment forecast) and ~134mn smartphones (−12%)**. The supply bridge: 2026 DRAM+HBM supply plus 2027 additions, less HBM allocation, less server/AI ex-HBM DRAM allocation, less other non-server, leaves a **PC & smartphone residual shortfall of 19bn Gb — 13% of demand.** MS's caveat is honest: "these numbers are sensitive to assumptions on memory content per device, but our analysis indicates **demand rationing risk if the supply build does not accelerate.**"
- **The structural demand shift under it:** **server share of DRAM demand rises 37% (2023) → 59% (2028e)**; **enterprise SSDs jump 18% → 65% of NAND demand** — "the result is a structurally tighter market where **non-AI buyers may face weaker access to supply for an extended period.**" On price: **"Prices for memory have risen more than six-fold over the last year — a sharp discontinuity from the multi-decade price declines"** (price per gigabyte of DRAM "fell by around a factor of 10 every five years from 1957 to 2020"). And on the physics of the fix: "**Building memory fabrication plants takes years, so there is no quick response**"; MS's own supply-relief chart puts install → qualification → yield ramp → product allocation at **~a two-year process**.
- **★ THE ACCOUNTING ASYMMETRY — the cleanest statement of why this squeeze does not hit all buyers equally, and it is the mechanism behind the [[AAPL]]-vs-hyperscaler split already on this page:** "**There is accounting asymmetry, where hyperscalers can CAPITALIZE memory-heavy AI servers and depreciate them over time, while consumer hardware and industrial OEMs see memory inflation flow more directly through inventory and COGS.**" The affected group is "companies that are **not directly monetizing AI but still have to pay AI-driven memory prices**," who must choose between passing on price increases, de-speccing devices, or accepting lower profits. MS's conclusion is the line worth keeping: "**Memory inflation is not just a financial transfer from non-AI sectors to memory producers. It is increasingly a constraint on what gets built and when for many non-tech industries.**" Sectors named beyond PCs and smartphones: gaming consoles, servers, cloud, networking, autos, medical devices, broadband equipment, industrial systems and low-cost education/embedded devices.
- **LTAs and the buyer hierarchy:** "**Long-Term Agreements are now absolute necessities to secure capacity**" — memory producers are establishing LTAs with all hyperscale customers, which lets them "**lock in long-term orders and prices, establish a stable high-margin model, and mitigate the industry's cyclical volatility.**" Allocation priority runs **HBM → server DRAM → enterprise SSD** ahead of consumer/industrial, who face "significantly higher prices, longer lead times and weaker bargaining power." Forward risk MS flags as *additive*, not priced: "**mobile AI agents and physical AI agents** — another very large additional surge in demand for memory chips is likely as these are rolled out."
- **US semis view on price (Joseph Moore, same note):** "**We see prices up at least 25% on a like-for-like basis from 2Q to 3Q, above our estimates and above 3rd-party estimates**," with "**no signs of shortages abating**" from data-center purchasing contacts "despite some concerns of a second derivative deceleration, higher capex, and de-speccing." Ratings from the note: **[[MU]] PT $1,200 (30x through-cycle EPS of $40); [[SAMSUNG]] OW PT KRW381,000 (Top Pick Korea); [[SKHYNIX]] OW PT KRW2,600,000.** (Morgan Stanley, 2026-07-27) [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Morgan_Stanley_700HK_Global_Thematics_Playing_the_AI_Infrastructure_Dip_Whe.html)
- **⚠ THE HYPERSCALER PRICING-POWER QUESTION, NOW WITH TWO DIFFERENT ANSWERS FROM THE TWO LARGEST BUYERS (2026-07-30).** Whether memory inflation actually lands on the memory buyers depends on whether they can reprice their own output, and the two hyperscalers answered differently on the same day. **[[MSFT]]'s IR (via the UBS callback): pricing is NOT locked over the life of long-term Azure contracts — frontier-lab pricing resets by capacity tranche ("there can absolutely be a pricing step-up as each tranche goes live"), enterprise discount-off-list "can alter throughout the contract," and MSFT has "secured hardware pricing via long-term deals" so it is "not as exposed to spot increases as one might think."** **[[AMZN]]'s Jassy, on the call: signed deals hold their signed price "over the duration of that contract," and "new agreements that you sign, you always take into account what your costs are" — recovery only on NEW business, with the acknowledgement that "there are inflated prices right now on some of the components like memory and hard drives and SSDs."** **Read together: Microsoft claims an in-contract reset mechanism, Amazon describes recovery only at renewal. If both are accurate, AWS carries more of the memory-inflation risk inside its existing book than Azure does — a genuine, testable divergence for the FY27 renewals.** (UBS callback + AMZN Q2'26 call, 2026-07-30)

## Recent signals (Jul 30, evening) — the demand-side proof arrives from OUTSIDE the memory complex, and the LTA framework becomes the new debate
- **★ [[AAPL]] is now the single best public datapoint on memory pricing power, and it came from a customer, not a supplier (AAPL FQ3 FY26 call, 2026-07-30).** Management's own attribution: memory costs explained **"more than 100% of"** the sequential gross-margin decline (49.3% → ~48.1% underlying), the Sept-quarter underlying GM guide of **~46.5%** is "also mostly explained by memory cost pressure," and beyond September the carry-in-inventory cushion fades while market pricing keeps rising, so memory becomes a **bigger** headwind. Cook's characterisation: a **"hundred-year flood on the memory pricing with exponential increases in memory prices"**, and price rises were taken **"reluctantly."** Apple also went public with wanting to break the oligopoly: *"primarily the DRAM market has three suppliers. Obviously, if there were more suppliers, that would be good. It would help us on the supply side and **perhaps** the pricing side … we're evaluating all options."* The market read it correctly — **[[MU]] +~3% and [[SNDK]] +~5% in the after-market on Apple's call** (JPM · Joshua Meyers, 2026-07-30). Note also **Apple's days of inventory rose to 18 from 11 q/q, read as memory stockpiling** (MS · Wigg).
- **★ [[AMZN]] raised FY26 capex ~$200B → ~$220B and attributed the increment to higher memory costs** (AMZN Q2 FY26 call, 2026-07-30; MS · Wigg). Memory inflation is now large enough to move a hyperscaler's capital budget by ~$20bn — the clearest dollar-denominated confirmation that pricing is being **passed through**, not resisted.
- **★ THE NEW DEBATE: LTAs are simultaneously the bull case and the cap on it. [[SAMSUNG]] disclosed 60-70% of future DRAM *and* NAND capacity under long-term agreements — the highest of any supplier (JPM · Jay Kwon, 2026-07-30).** Full terms: **five-year base contracts with an option to extend a further year annually; floor prices firmly set; five major CSP contracts completed with five more major AI-customer contracts in the pipeline; more than a quarter of total LTA prepayment already collected as of end-2Q26.** And the market's reaction tells you how to read it — Samsung rallied to **+4.6%** intraday on foundry ([[AVGO]] named, 2nm design wins to more than double y/y) and on management saying the shortage **worsens in 2027 and stays tight through 2028**, then **faded to close -3.7%** on the LTA number. Jefferies' Conor O'Mara has the mechanism: "that **limits the optionality on the upside in pricing** as we go into next year. Investors from the US said **[[MU]]'s comments on LTAs capped its upside and maybe that is happening to Samsung too**." TrendForce frames the same fact as strategy: "aiming to allocate **up to 70% capacity to LTAs**, Samsung could be taking a **more aggressive stance on long-term AI memory supply than Micron and SK hynix**."
- **★ The SK hynix "miss" is now explained, and the explanation is a mix effect that doubles as the moat (BTG Pactual expert call · Hongkyo Jung, former SK Hynix executive, 2026-07-30).** **[[SKHYNIX]] runs ~40% of wafer capacity on HBM vs [[SAMSUNG]]'s maximum ~25%.** HBM prices are set roughly a year in advance and do not track spot, so when conventional DRAM spiked, SK hynix's blended DRAM ASP printed **+30% against Samsung's +mid-40%** — Samsung captured the spike, SK hynix was contracted through it. Margin structure behind that: **HBM earns a stable 40-60%, while conventional advanced server DRAM can exceed 80% during sharp price increases.** But the yield gap is the durable fact: **SK hynix HBM4 yield ~80-85% ("matured," similar to prior generations) vs Samsung ~60%** — worth ~40% of potential output to Samsung, and the reason SK hynix can supply Nvidia's Rubin volumes. Pricing ladder confirmed: **HBM3E $300-400 → HBM4 $500-600** on double the I/Os. **SK hynix Q2 operating margin 76%, up from 72%** — "exceeding margins seen in companies like TSMC or Apple." Forward: expects **Q3 DRAM increases to moderate to +13-18% q/q** from Q2's +30%. Next-gen differentiation: **hybrid bonding** and **3D DRAM**. On China: "Chinese memory makers are **effectively blocked from competing in advanced DRAM/HBM due to US export controls on critical manufacturing equipment**, cementing the market dominance of the 'big three'."
- **HBM4 ramp — both Korean suppliers now guiding to a 3x-type step, but from different starting points.** [[SAMSUNG]]: **HBM4 (12Hi) sales up 3x q/q in 3Q26, >60% of HBM revenue in 2H26/2026; HBM4E samples already shipped to major customers; targets HBM market share in line with its overall DRAM share in 2027 (JPMe mid-to-high 30%); HBM capacity to ~280K by end-2027 from 220K at end-2026** (JPM Kwon; MS · Shawn Kim; Susquehanna · Hosseini, all 2026-07-30). [[SKHYNIX]]: HBM4 mass-production shipments **started in 2Q26** with the ramp in 2H26, HBM4 yield already approaching mature HBM3E levels, **HBM4E samples delivered in 1H26 with mass production targeted 2027**, and hybrid bonding / iHBM in development for HBM5 (@SKundojjala, @QQ_Timmy, 2026-07-29). Supply-chain confirmation: "**South Korean suppliers win HBM4 tester orders from SK Hynix**" (DIGITIMES, 2026-07-30) — read with [[ADVANTEST]]'s beat-and-raise + TAM raise and pulled-forward capacity.
- **★ 2027 forks: DRAM stays tight, NAND is genuinely contested (TrendForce, 2026-07-30 vs [[SAMSUNG]] management, same day).** TrendForce: DRAM — most new fab wafer starts only ramp in 2H27 with meaningful output in 2028, and **HBM consumes far more wafers per bit so added starts don't convert proportionally to bits**; sufficiency gap **~-1% to -2% in 2026, widening in 2027**; consumer (phone/notebook) shipments decline on higher prices. NAND — 2027 sees accelerated high-layer-count adoption plus new fab output, **bit supply growth exceeds the prior year, sufficiency turns positive and the market loosens in 2H27 with prices at risk of correction.** **Samsung management said the opposite on NAND the same day:** inventory remains significantly low amid surging **KV-cache** demand and it expects NAND supply-demand to **worsen** next year — JPM: "this **debunks** the recent industry concerns about NAND order cuts, piling inventory, and supply outpacing demand in 2027." Samsung specifics: **QLC bit growth to double 2H vs 1H, server SSD mix >60% in 2026, V10 NAND (300+ layer) using hybrid copper bonding into mass production Aug-26.** *This is a live, unresolved disagreement — flagged deliberately.*
- **Citi memory update call (2026-07-28 21:00, via @MF_Camillus 2026-07-29) — the sell-side price deck.** "The recent correction across memory stocks appears driven by **excessive market pessimism**, and risk/reward is becoming increasingly attractive." Key numbers: **NAND supplier inventories are just 2.6 weeks versus a more typical ~5 weeks**, with the Big 3 prioritising DRAM/HBM capacity while AI workloads (QLC SSDs, KV cache, inference storage) drive NAND — tight through 2027. **Server DRAM: Citi projects 64GB module prices rising from ~$1,300 in 2Q to $1,600 in 3Q and $1,800 in 4Q.** "Most of the market's concerns remain centered on **Chinese consumer demand rather than the enterprise/AI segment**." AI to become the dominant NAND demand driver with server >[half] of demand.
- **Supply-side hard constraint, restated by the supplier:** [[SAMSUNG]] management — **"new fabs require more than three years from construction to wafer production, creating a bottleneck to meaningful capacity additions through at least 2028"**, and "**we will not see any meaningful increase in supply through 2028**"; spillover demand from 2026 carries into 2027, "which is why [we] think supply demand will be **even tighter in 2027 and still tight in 2028**." Also: "**AI frontier model developers are approaching Samsung directly with requests for multi-year agreements**"; "cloud memory capacity is booked out." Discipline: "building out clean rooms first then moving in equipment based on near-term demand" (Susquehanna · Hosseini; JEF · O'Mara, 2026-07-30). Independent corroboration from an ex-NVIDIA expert: "**key memory suppliers (Micron, Hynix, Samsung) have large contracts locked in until 2028 at 2026 pricing**" (AlphaSense/StoneX, 2026-07-30).
- **China: [[MU]]'s competitive risk became a political event this week.** **CXMT's IPO rose 466% on debut to become the No.1 company in the China stock market** (WSJ via Sinocism, 2026-07-30); US lawmakers are seeking a national-security probe ("ticking time bomb," NY Post); **SemiAnalysis published "China's CXMT Is Set to Challenge DRAM Incumbents"**; and **Apple is actively lobbying to relax restrictions on US companies working with PRC memory firms** — WSJ: "**Trump Caught Between Apple and Micron in Fight Over Chinese Chips**." Against Apple: a bipartisan Senate group led by **Banks (R-IN) and Schumer (D-NY)** urging Apple to abandon CXMT and YMTC, both on an updated Pentagon list (Bloomberg via @firstadopter, 2026-07-29). Also on the table the same week: **"High memory prices could slow China's AI push"** (DIGITIMES) and **@jukan05: "CXMT may soon catch up to SK hynix in market capitalization"** (2026-07-29) — with CXMT at 10.5x earnings vs SK hynix at 3.0x (@JulianKlymochko, 2026-07-29).
- **Valuation dislocation, for the record.** Post the Korea selloff, **[[MU]] and [[SNDK]] trade around ~5x FY2 earnings versus [[NVDA]] at ~15x** (@The_AI_Investor, 2026-07-30). @MF_Camillus's version: "memory stocks trade below 5x NTM P/E because supposedly this same AI wave is nothing but pure hype … it's a clown's market" (2026-07-29). And the mechanical explanation for a chunk of the July drawdown landed on 07-30: CNBC reported **Leopold Aschenbrenner's Situational Awareness fund was forced to unwind its entire public book to Citadel in a single print** — his largest holdings bounced hardest (**NBIS +27%, BE +27%, SNDK +26%, CRWV +22%**), i.e. forced deleveraging, not a fundamental re-rating (TMT Breakout EOD, 2026-07-30).

## What it is / why it matters
Memory has gone from the cyclical victim of the semi complex to its tightest, most profitable corner. HBM — the stacked DRAM that feeds AI accelerators — is **sold out for all of CY26 on price and volume, including HBM4** ([MU page](../MU.md); Mehrotra, Micron Q1 FY26, 2025-12-17), and that scarcity has spread back into conventional DRAM and NAND because every HBM die displaces ~3x the wafer of a commodity DDR5 die. The result is a pricing melt-up that has run the memory names parabolic (MU +30% in a week, $748→$971; Susquehanna 2026-05-30 via [MU briefing](../_briefings/by-ticker/MU.md)) and re-rated the equity story from "trough multiple on cyclical earnings" toward an earnings-durability framework underwritten by multi-year contracts. The buy-side question this theme resolves: **is this a structurally longer up-cycle (LTAs + supply discipline + AI memory-intensity), or the textbook commodity top that always follows astronomical ASPs?** The on-disk evidence (May–Jun 2026) is two-sided and the debate is no longer direction but duration.

## State of play (latest)
- **★ NEW (2026-08-25) — THE PER-GW DRAM WAFER BILL GETS A NUMBER, AND FOR ONCE THIS PAGE'S OWN INPUTS CONFIRM IT: ~170K DRAM WAFERS PER GW OF VERA RUBIN.** **Dylan Patel (SemiAnalysis)** puts the per-GW wafer bill at **55,000 N3 + 6,000 N5 + 170,000 DRAM wafers per GW of Vera Rubin**. **Source: Dylan Patel (SemiAnalysis), in interview — relayed by Felipe, 2026-08-25.** ⚠️ **VINTAGE, not attribution, is the open item: Patel dates the figures to an interview “a few months” earlier, so carry them as ~2Q26 vintage rather than a fresh mark — which matters because the SOCAMM spec has been halved since (see the sensitivity below).** **House bottom-up on this page's own numbers (Capstone, 2026-08-25), at ~4,545 racks/GW (VR200 at 220-225kW — Bernstein and TrendForce agree):** per rack, **72 packages × 288GB HBM4 = 20.7TB** plus **36 Vera CPUs × 768GB SOCAMM = 27.6TB** (post-cut spec; the pre-cut 1.5TB/CPU is what makes NVDA's official *75TB of fast memory per NVL144* add up as 20.7 + 54). Converting at **MU's own displacement ratio — HBM3E ~3:1 vs DDR5 for the same bits, worsening to ~4:1 by HBM4E (2026-08-15, this page)** — and ~2,450 GB per conventional/LPDDR wafer: **HBM 25-34 wafers/rack + SOCAMM 11 wafers/rack ⇒ 167-205k DRAM wafers/GW on the all-288GB SKU, or 141-170k on JPM's assumption that 70% of SKUs take the downsized 192GB spec.** The quoted **170k sits mid-corridor** — i.e. the number is right, and it is the *conventional/LPDDR* leg that carries a third of it, consistent with DIGITIMES' **memory = ~62% of the Vera Rubin BOM, led by SOCAMM2 rather than HBM4 (2026-08-11)**. ⚠️ **Two consequences for this page.** First, **the denominator is proved, not assumed**: on a FACILITY-GW frame (Redburn's 3,130 racks/GW) the same 170k would require **~480GB per HBM wafer — a ~5:1 silicon displacement vs DDR5, worse than MU's own ~4:1 at HBM4E** — so the facility-GW reading is unlikely though not physically impossible; treat this as an IT-load/compute-TDP GW card and never net it against facility-GW figures without restating it. Second, **the spec cuts this page has been tracking move it materially**: on the pre-cut 1.5TB SOCAMM the DRAM leg would be **~215-235k/GW**, and on an all-192GB HBM SKU mix it falls to **~130-150k/GW** — a **±30% band driven entirely by the de-spec debate**, which is why any 'DRAM wafers per GW' figure has to be stamped with the HBM SKU and the SOCAMM spec it assumes. Cross-ref [semicap-wfe.md](semicap-wfe.md) (Bernstein's 46-50k WSPM/GW — standing CAPACITY, a different denominator), [../TSM.md](../TSM.md) (the logic leg, where the quoted 55k N3 is ~2x a die-area bottom-up), ledger [`wafers-per-gw`](../_meta/assumptions.md).
- **★ NEW (2026-07-29/30) — 🔴 THE DEMAND-DESTRUCTION SIDE OF THE SHORTAGE IS NOW VISIBLE IN THREE SEPARATE P&Ls ON THE SAME NIGHT, AND IT IS THE CLEANEST EVIDENCE ON THIS PAGE THAT THE MEMORY UPCYCLE IS BEING PAID FOR AT THE HANDSET LAYER.** The page has extensive coverage of who is *earning* the memory shortage; this is the first datapoint set showing exactly where the cost lands. All three printed 2026-07-29/30:
  - **[[QCOM]] (F3Q26, 07-29 AMC) — management named it in the press release.** Cristiano Amon: "**Despite a challenging memory and supply environment**, our third quarter results reflect solid execution." The September guide came in **below consensus at the EPS midpoint** ($2.05-2.25 vs $2.38) and the stock fell **-5.9% AH**. GS is more specific and adds a second leg: the weak guide reflects "persistent smartphone headwinds driven by **memory-related demand destruction and deteriorating business at Apple**." (UBS · Robert Ruple desk, 2026-07-29; GS GIR via GS TMT Today, 2026-07-30)
  - **[[ARM]] (F1Q27, 07-29 AMC) — the same mechanism, quantified.** ARM **cut its FY27 royalty revenue growth outlook to high-teens % y/y from ~20%**, which GS attributes to "near-term pressure in the smartphone market" driven by **memory cost-induced demand destruction**. Note the shape: royalties are a **unit-linked** revenue line, so a royalty-growth cut is a fairly direct read on handset *units* being lost to BOM inflation — arguably the cleanest unit-elasticity datapoint available on this page. (GS GIR via GS TMT Today, 2026-07-30)

(AP via GS TMT Today, 2026-07-30)
  - **Why it matters for the durability debate on this page:** Samsung simultaneously guided the market to stay **undersupplied** — "expects demand for its memory products to **remain strong in the second half**… **demand for server chips is expected to accelerate further, keeping the market undersupplied**" on AI infrastructure and **agentic AI** adoption. So the two forces are now explicit and pulling opposite ways: **server/AI demand is inelastic and accelerating, while handset demand is visibly elastic and already cracking.** That asymmetry — not aggregate bit demand — is what determines whether the pricing runs longer. Same morning, **UBS initiated [[SKHYNIX]] at Buy** and JPM argued "**finally, a case for Memory on P/B**" with "Korea positioning: unwind largely done" (JPM · Matthew See, 2026-07-30).
- **NEW (2026-07-28) — 🔴 THE HBM COST STEP-UP NOW HAS BOTH SIDES OF THE COMPARISON AND A NON-NVDA PRICE POINT, PLUS TWO STRUCTURAL MECHANISMS THE PAGE DID NOT CARRY (Fubon "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates" · Sherman Shang / Daniel Yang, 2026-07-28; Micron IR call · Satish, 2026-07-27; Morgan Stanley webcast "2Q26 memory, Intel implications and ARM preview" · Sean, 2026-07-28).**
  - **THE PRICE LADDER, COMPLETED. The US$31-32/GB HBM4 figure already on this page (via the Jefferies relay in the bullet below) is one leg of a three-leg comparison in the primary Fubon text, and the other two are what make it interpretable.** Verbatim: **"nVidia's HBM 4 cost will increase to US$31-32 per GB from US$17-18 per GB (HBM 3e) in 2026… For other GPU and custom-ASIC projects, we believe their HBM cost will be higher than nVidia's and may reach US$35-36 per GB, meaning the HBM cost will double in 2027."** **Three reads. (1) The NVDA step is ~+80% per GB, not a doubling — the "doubling" claim is an INDUSTRY statement anchored on the US$35-36 non-NVDA price, so do not apply "HBM cost doubles" to NVDA's own BOM. (2) There is now an explicit ~US$4/GB NVDA PROCUREMENT DISCOUNT versus everyone else — a quantified scale/priority advantage that the merchant-vs-custom debate on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md) has been arguing qualitatively for months. (3) The cost flows straight to price: Fubon estimates "nVidia's Rubin GPU price will skyrocket to US$78-80K assuming nVidia is still able to maintain its GM at 75-80% in chips"** — i.e. **the accelerator ASP inflation the market is calling "AI inflation" is substantially an HBM pass-through, held-margin, not a margin grab.** _(Enrichment of the existing 07-28 Jefferies relay bullet — the US$31-32/GB and 1.03mn-wafer marks were already logged and are NOT double-counted here.)_ [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Our_advanced_packaging_CoWoS_forecast_adjustments_other_up.html)
  - **MECHANISM 1 — HBM HAS NEVER TRADED AS A COMMODITY, AND MICRON SAYS SO DIRECTLY. This is the cleanest primary-source answer on the page to "is this time different?"** Satish, asked exactly that: **"if you look at memory and you see what we make today, there is a broader diversity of products than ever before… For example, HBM, it's a product where for the last three years, we've had annual contracts for these products for volumes and price. And there is no market where there's HBM on a spot market or quarterly market type business. It's more on a business that you get a PO, we make the product type business."** **Three years of annual volume-AND-price contracts with no spot or quarterly market is a structural fact, not a cycle observation — it means the DRAM spot/contract series this page tracks is simply not a read on HBM economics, and the second-derivative pricing debate raging on [macro-cycle.md](macro-cycle.md) does not mechanically transmit to the HBM line.** He frames the SCA broadening (automotive first, now across the portfolio) as the same pattern spreading. **MECHANISM 2 — HBM IS NAMED AS A CAUSE OF THE INDUSTRY BIT SHORTAGE, NOT JUST A BENEFICIARY OF IT:** among the structural factors constraining supply growth Satish lists **"the time it takes to build greenfield capacity, the new nodes not producing as much bit growth, or new products such as H[B]M consuming capacity."** **HBM is therefore endogenous to the commodity DRAM squeeze — capacity it absorbs is capacity conventional DRAM does not get.** And on the demand side, Micron published **two white papers in the last few months** arguing **"if you increase either the content or bandwidth of HBM 4, you improve the throughput"** of tokens (the LPDDR analogue: more content → more token throughput) — **the vendor's own attempt to make memory content a token-economics variable rather than a BOM line** (see [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md)). (Micron IR call · Satish, 2026-07-27) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_27_mu_ir_call_27_jul_26.html)
  - **MECHANISM 3 — THE HBM-vs-COMMODITY-DRAM SUPPLY SUBSTITUTION, STATED AS A SPECIFIC 2026-27 DRIVER. MS's Sean, explaining why TrendForce keeps raising DRAM bit growth (25% → 31% this year, ~24% next year): "Some of that could be due to Samsung P4 second floor clean room, as well as Hynix probably HBM3E, where no HBM4 will actually reduce the amount of total HBM and more DRAM will be produced as a result."** **Read the arithmetic, because it is the underappreciated leg of the HBM4-delay story this page has been tracking since the SK Hynix Q2 miss: HBM4 consumes materially more wafer per delivered bit than HBM3E, so [[SKHYNIX]] running HBM3E instead of HBM4 MECHANICALLY FREES WAFERS INTO COMMODITY DRAM.** **That makes the HBM4 ramp delay a double-edged event — bearish the HBM revenue line and the leading-edge mix, but bearish commodity DRAM pricing too, via supply. The page previously carried the HBM4 delay only as an HBM-side timing miss.** ⚠️ Sean's transcript renders HBM as "HPM" throughout; read as HBM. (Morgan Stanley webcast · Sean, 2026-07-28) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_28_2q26_ms_memory_intel_implications_and_arm_preview.html)

Put differently, the HDD-to-eSSD acquisition cost advantage remains approximately 17-20x today (VS SUBSTITUTION RISK AT 3x), and is LIKELY TO WIDEN FURTHER"** — MS's Exhibit 4 has the differential **"expected to exceed 20x exiting CY26."** Three conclusions MS draws from it, all relevant here: **"(1) any near-term concerns of NAND pricing are overblown, (2) the substitution risk of eSSD gets smaller by the day (though still relevant medium to longer-term), and (3) HDD's should continue to see upward pressure on pricing."** **Why this matters for the memory page: it is an independent, price-based test of the NAND-weakness bear — MS says explicitly that "concerns linking potential NAND pricing weakness to HDD pricing are MISPLACED," and its checks show tightening rather than softening.** Cross-check against what is already on this page: the Bernstein ex-WDC NAND VP put the **TCO crossover at ~1.5-3x $/GB** with the gap having blown out to **~15-25x** (2026-05-04) — **MS's 17-20x and 3x threshold are consistent with that framework, but the DIRECTION disagrees: Bernstein read a swing back toward HDD as "somewhat inevitable," while MS has the gap WIDENING through CY26.** Both are on file; the resolution is whether eSSD $/GB falls faster than nearline $/TB rises. **The timing analogue, which is the second thing to carry: "MEMORY PRICING DID NOT BEGIN ITS SHARP UPWARD MOVE UNTIL SEPTEMBER OF LAST YEAR — HDD pricing negotiations might not turn aggressive immediately after memory contract pricing increase"** — MS uses the memory cycle as the lead indicator for HDD contract negotiations, i.e. a stated lag of roughly a year between the memory inflection and the storage tier repricing. **The demand frame under it, for sizing the AI-data build-out below HBM: industry checks indicate a 300-400EB HDD SUPPLY SHORTFALL EVERY YEAR THROUGH CY28; CSPs are now requesting CAPACITY VISIBILITY THROUGH 2032; CY27-CY28 pricing discussions are "increasingly moving towards $25-30/TB"; and out-of-contract "spot" EBs command a 30-40%+ PREMIUM to contracted volumes while representing only ~15-20% OF NEARLINE EB SHIPMENTS** — the last figure is the one that caps how much the spot premium can flow into blended pricing, and is MS's own reason for rejecting 10%+ q/q nearline increases in the September quarter. (Morgan Stanley · Erik Woodring "Hard Disk Drives — F4Q26 Earnings Preview", 2026-07-27; see [../WDC.md](../WDC.md), [../SNDK.md](../SNDK.md), [../KIOXIA.md](../KIOXIA.md), [../MU.md](../MU.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/ITHARDWARE_20260727_0401.html)

WE THINK THIS IS THE SAME MISTAKE BEING MADE TWICE: READING AN EFFICIENCY GAIN IN ATTENTION MEMORY AS A CUT TO MEMORY DEMAND.' The KV compression (~75%, versus DeepSeek V4's ~90%) IS WHAT MAKES OFFLOAD ECONOMICALLY VIABLE IN THE FIRST PLACE, WHICH EXPANDS THE NAND/DRAM OPPORTUNITY RATHER THAN SHRINKING IT.'** Demand-side check the same week: **'procurement remains tight, LTAs are doing more of the work, and DRAM/NAND PRICE STRENGTH IS SPREADING BEYOND SPOT'** — which, if right, makes the spot-price datapoint that drove Friday the least informative part of the picture.
